Cost is .95*(1-cos_sim(names)) +\n    # .05*range_cost, so with ranges that agree this is a bound on naming:\n    # .5 requires cos_sim >= ~.47, i.e. names that are at least loosely related.\n    # It was 1, which nothing could fail -- unrelated names with matching ranges\n    # score ~.95, so only an inf (dimension mismatch) ever tripped it.\n    'input_match_cost': .5,\n    'output_match_cost': 1, # output match mean cost must be below this\n    'input_range_error': .2, # max error between input range limits\n    'output_range_error': .9 # max error between output range limits\n}\nMIN_ACCURACY_FRAC = 0.95 # submitted accuracy must be at least this fraction of reference accuracy for each output dimension\n\n\ndef match_outputs(submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats):\n    \"\"\"Single source of truth for matching submitted output variables to\n    reference output variables. Used by both test_data_stats (for the\n    output-cost / range-error / fraction-error metrics) and\n    test_decoder_accuracy (to know which submitted accuracy maps to which\n    reference accuracy when computing the ratio). Keeping one call site\n    ensures both tests see the same assignment.\"\"\"\n    return match_variables_by_hungarian(\n        submitted_data_stats['output_range'],\n        reference_data_stats['data_summary']['output_range'],\n        submitted_data_stats['output_fractions'],\n        reference_data_stats['data_summary']['output_fractions'],\n        weight_semantic=.5 * 19 / 20,\n        weight_range=.5,\n        weight_fraction=.5 / 20,\n        exact_range=False,\n    )\n\ndef test_data_stats(metrics, submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats):\n\n    fields_check = [\n        'nsessions',\n        'ntrials_total',\n        'T_median',\n        'nsubjects',\n        'nneurons_total',\n    ]\n\n    # Always record the raw submitted counts, even when there's no reference to\n    # compare against — the numbers themselves are useful for downstream\n    # reporting / grids.\n    for field in fields_check:\n        metrics[field] = submitted_data_stats[field]\n\n    # Record per-variable n_classes for the agent's outputs. We do this BEFORE\n    # the reference-availability check because the value doesn't depend on a\n    # reference, and downstream (e.g. chance-baselined / informedness plots)\n    # need it for unsupervised tasks too.\n    sub_output_range = submitted_data_stats.get('output_range', {})\n    for var, (lo, hi) in sub_output_range.items():\n        metrics[f'output_nclasses_{var}'] = int(round(hi - lo + 1))\n\n    # Per-variable input range — also recorded BEFORE the skip so unsupervised\n    # tasks have it. Inputs aren't necessarily integer-coded, so we record the\n    # endpoints as [lo, hi] rather than an n_classes count.\n    sub_input_range = submitted_data_stats.get('input_range', {})\n    for var, (lo, hi) in sub_input_range.items():\n        metrics[f'input_range_{var}'] = [float(lo), float(hi)]\n\n    # Also record the raw counts (dinput / doutput) — same rationale.\n    metrics['dinput']  = int(submitted_data_stats.get('dinput', 0))\n    metrics['doutput'] = int(submitted_data_stats.get('doutput', 0))\n\n    if reference_data_stats is None:\n        pytest.skip(\"Reference data stats not available for comparison\")\n        return\n\n    for field in fields_check:\n        metrics[field+'_ratio'] = submitted_data_stats[field] / reference_data_stats['data_summary'][field]\n\n    # check that input ranges match via 1-1 matching\n    input_matches, input_match_costs = match_variables_by_hungarian(\n        submitted_data_stats['input_range'],\n        reference_data_stats['data_summary']['input_range'],\n        weight_semantic=.95,\n        weight_range=.05,\n        weight_fraction=0,\n        exact_range=False,\n    )\n\n    # A task with no decoder inputs (dinput == 0) is valid: there is nothing to\n    # match, so there is no mismatch cost. np.mean([]) would be NaN, and every\n    # comparison against NaN is False, so the cost assertion below could never pass.\n    # Named for what it is: the mean Hungarian MATCHING cost, which is dominated\n    # by name similarity and is forced to 0 for exactly-matching names before any\n    # range term is computed. It is not a mean of the input_range_error_<var>\n    # fields -- that is mean_input_range_error, recorded below.\n    mean_input_cost = float(np.mean(input_match_costs)) if len(input_match_costs) else 0.0\n    metrics['input_match_mean_cost'] = mean_input_cost\n    metrics['input_matches'] = [\n        {'reference': ref, 'submitted': sub, 'cost': cost}\n        for ref, sub, cost in input_matches\n    ]\n    sub_input_range = submitted_data_stats['input_range']\n    ref_input_range = reference_data_stats['data_summary']['input_range']\n    for ref_name, sub_name, _ in input_matches:\n        ref_lo, ref_hi = ref_input_range[ref_name]\n        sub_lo, sub_hi = sub_input_range[sub_name]\n        scale = abs(ref_hi - ref_lo)\n        if scale < 1e-6 and abs(sub_hi - sub_lo) < 1e-6:\n            metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] = 0.0\n        else:\n            scale = max(scale, 1e-6)\n            metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] = float(max(abs(ref_lo - sub_lo)/scale, abs(ref_hi - sub_hi)/scale))\n    mean_input_range_error = np.mean([metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] for ref_name, _, _ in input_matches]) if len(input_matches) else 0.0\n    metrics['mean_input_range_error'] = mean_input_range_error\n\n    # match outputs based on range, fraction, and semantic similarity of names\n    output_matches, output_match_costs = match_outputs(\n        submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats\n    )\n    metrics['output_matches'] = [\n        {'reference': ref, 'submitted': sub, 'cost': cost}\n        for ref, sub, cost in output_matches\n    ]\n\n    # As above: the mean matching cost, not a mean of output_fraction_error_<var>.\n    mean_output_match_cost = float(np.mean(output_match_costs))\n    metrics['output_match_mean_cost'] = mean_output_match_cost\n    sub_output_range = submitted_data_stats['output_range']\n    ref_output_range = reference_data_stats['data_summary']['output_range']\n    for ref_name, sub_name, _ in output_matches:\n        ref_lo, ref_hi = ref_output_range[ref_name]\n        sub_lo, sub_hi = sub_output_range[sub_name]\n        metrics[f'output_range_error_{ref_name}'] = float(max(abs(ref_lo - sub_lo), abs(ref_hi - sub_hi)))\n        # Record the number of output classes per variable, so consumers can\n        # derive chance level (= 1 / n_classes) for chance-baselined metrics.\n        # For integer-coded categorical outputs, n_classes = hi - lo + 1.\n        # `output_nclasses_<var>` is the submitted (agent) value; the\n        # `_reference_<var>` variant is the reference's n_classes.\n        metrics[f'output_nclasses_{ref_name}'] = int(round(sub_hi - sub_lo + 1))\n        metrics[f'output_nclasses_reference_{ref_name}'] = int(round(ref_hi - ref_lo + 1))\n        # fraction error per output\n        ref_fracs = sorted(reference_data_stats['data_summary']['output_fractions'][ref_name].values())\n        sub_fracs = sorted(submitted_data_stats['output_fractions'][sub_name].values())\n        if len(ref_fracs) == len(sub_fracs):\n            metrics[f'output_fraction_error_{ref_name}'] = float(np.sum(np.abs(np.array(ref_fracs) - np.array(sub_fracs))))\n        else:\n            metrics[f'output_fraction_error_{ref_name}'] = None\n\n    for field in fields_check:\n        assert 1-STATLIMITS[field+'_ratio'] <= metrics[field+'_ratio'] <= 1+STATLIMITS[field+'_ratio'], f\"{field} in submitted data ({submitted_data_stats[field]}) is not within {STATLIMITS[field+'_ratio']*100}% of reference ({reference_data_stats['data_summary'][field]})\"\n\n    # check that the number of inputs and outputs matches the reference\n    assert submitted_data_stats['dinput'] == reference_data_stats['data_summary']['dinput'], f\"dinput in submitted data ({submitted_data_stats['dinput']}) does not match reference ({reference_data_stats['data_summary']['dinput']})\"\n    assert submitted_data_stats['doutput'] == reference_data_stats['data_summary']['doutput'], f\"doutput in submitted data ({submitted_data_stats['doutput']}) does not match reference ({reference_data_stats['data_summary']['doutput']})\"\n\n    # check that we could match inputs with a reasonably low cost, indicating that their names and ranges are similar\n    assert mean_input_cost < STATLIMITS['input_match_cost'], (\n        f\"Input range mean matching cost too high ({mean_input_cost:.3f} >= {STATLIMITS['input_match_cost']}). \"\n        f\"Matches: {[(r, s, f'{c:.3f}') for r, s, c in input_matches]}\"\n    )\n    \n    # check that input ranges match\n    if len(input_matches) > 0:\n        assert metrics['mean_input_range_error'] <= STATLIMITS['input_range_error'], f\"Mean input range error ({metrics['mean_input_range_error']:.3f}) exceeds allowed limit ({STATLIMITS['input_range_error']})\"\n\n    # check that outputs match\n    inf_matches = [(r, s) for r, s, c in output_matches if np.isinf(c)]\n    assert not inf_matches, (\n        f\"Output ranges do not match exactly for: {inf_matches}. \"\n        f\"All matches: {[(r, s, f'{c:.3f}') for r, s, c in output_matches]}\"\n    )\n    \n    # change that output ranges match\n    for ref_name, sub_name, _ in output_matches:\n        assert metrics[f'output_range_error_{ref_name}'] <= STATLIMITS['output_range_error'], f\"Output range error for {sub_name} ({metrics[f'output_range_error_{ref_name}']:.3f}) exceeds allowed limit ({STATLIMITS['output_range_error']})\"\n\n    # check that outputs match by name, range and distribution\n    assert mean_output_match_cost < STATLIMITS['output_match_cost'], (\n        f\"Output mean matching cost too high ({mean_output_match_cost:.3f} >= {STATLIMITS['output_match_cost']}). \"\n        f\"Matches: {[(r, s, f'{c:.3f}') for r, s, c in output_matches]}\"\n    )\n\ndef test_decoder_accuracy(metrics, submitted_data_full, submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats):\n    \"\"\"Run the provided train_decoder.py script on the sample data and check that it runs without error.\"\"\"\n\n    train_params = {\n        'npcs': 100,\n        'lr': 1e-3,\n        'l1_weight': 1e-4,\n        'balanced_loss': True,\n    }\n\n    metrics['validation_balanced_accuracy'] = None\n    \n    if reference_data_stats is not None:\n        ref_accuracy = reference_data_stats['validation_balanced_accuracy']\n        metrics['validation_balanced_accuracy_reference'] = ref_accuracy\n        metrics['validation_balanced_accuracy_ratio'] = None\n\n    # train the decoder on the submitted data\n    rng_state = reference_data_stats.get(\"rng_state\") if reference_data_stats else None \n    try:\n        scores, predictions, pcs, confidences, train_idx, test_idx, model, output, _ = \\\n            train_validate_decoder(submitted_data_full['neural'], submitted_data_full['input'],\n                                submitted_data_full['output'], state=rng_state, **train_params)\n    except torch.cuda.OutOfMemoryError:\n        print(\"CUDA out of memory error during decoder training. Retrying on CPU...\")\n        train_params['device'] = torch.device('cpu')\n        scores, predictions, pcs, confidences, train_idx, test_idx, model, output, _ = \\\n            train_validate_decoder(submitted_data_full['neural'], submitted_data_full['input'],\n                                submitted_data_full['output'], state=rng_state, **train_params)\n\n    # evaluate accuracy on test set\n    test_predictions_only = get_trial_indices(predictions, test_idx)\n    test_output_only = get_trial_indices(output, test_idx)\n    balanced_accuracy = accuracy_all_sessions(test_predictions_only, test_output_only, balanced=True)\n\n    sub_accuracy = {\n        submitted_data_full['output_names'][i]: float(balanced_accuracy[i])\n        for i in range(len(balanced_accuracy))\n    }\n    # Persist the raw per-output accuracy as soon as it's computed. Everything\n    # below can raise or assert, so anything done afterwards must not be\n    # required to preserve this field.\n    metrics['validation_balanced_accuracy'] = sub_accuracy\n    \n    if reference_data_stats is None:\n        pytest.skip(\"Reference data stats not available for comparison, skipping accuracy ratio and threshold checks\")\n        return\n\n    # The matching + ratio + threshold checks below are wrapped so that a\n    # failure there doesn't prevent the raw accuracy above from being reported.\n    # Any error is re-raised at the end so the test still fails visibly, but\n    # not before the metric has been recorded.\n    deferred_error = None\n    try:\n        # find a match between output dimensions (same as test_data_stats)\n        output_matches, _ = match_outputs(\n            submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats\n        )\n        # The mean of those costs is deliberately NOT recorded here. test_data_stats\n        # already writes it as output_match_mean_cost from the same match_outputs\n        # call, and a second copy only drifts: this one is written solely by a\n        # decoder retrain, so after any reference change it lags the first. That is\n        # exactly what happened to sosa2024, where the old copy was averaged over\n        # the 3 outputs the reference had at the time rather than today's 6.\n\n        # Best-effort ratio: skip any match whose reference or submitted name\n        # isn't resolvable (e.g. matcher returned inf cost / None).\n        ratio = {}\n        for ref_name, sub_name, cost in output_matches:\n            if sub_name is None or sub_name not in sub_accuracy:\n                continue\n            if ref_name is None or ref_name not in ref_accuracy or not ref_accuracy[ref_name]:\n                continue\n            ratio[ref_name] = sub_accuracy[sub_name] / ref_accuracy[ref_name]\n        metrics['validation_balanced_accuracy_ratio'] = ratio\n\n        for ref_name, sub_name, _ in output_matches:\n            if sub_name is None or sub_name not in sub_accuracy:\n                continue\n            if ref_name is None or ref_name not in ref_accuracy:\n                continue\n            ref_acc = ref_accuracy[ref_name]\n            sub_acc = sub_accuracy[sub_name]\n            assert sub_acc >= MIN_ACCURACY_FRAC * ref_acc, f'Accuracy for output {sub_name} ({sub_acc:.3f}) is below {MIN_ACCURACY_FRAC*100:.1f}% of reference accuracy ({ref_acc:.3f})'\n    except BaseException as e:  # AssertionError or anything raised by the matcher\n        deferred_error = e\n\n    if deferred_error is not None:\n        raise deferred_error\n\n\n    \n","numLines":726,"startLine":1,"totalLines":726}}}

295→[![Coverage badge](https://img.shields.io/endpoint.svg?url=https%3A%2F%2Fibllib.hooks.internationalbrainlab.org%2Fcoverage%2Fibllib%2Fmaster)](https://ibllib.hooks.internationalbrainlab.org/coverage/master)\n   296→[![Tests status badge](https://img.shields.io/endpoint.svg?url=https%3A%2F%2Fibllib.hooks.internationalbrainlab.org%2Ftests%2Fibllib%2Fmaster)](https://ibllib.hooks.internationalbrainlab.org/logs/records/master)\n   297→[![Tests status badge](https://img.shields.io/endpoint?label=develop&url=https%3A%2F%2Fibllib.hooks.internationalbrainlab.org%2Ftests%2Fibllib%2Fdevelop)](https://ibllib.hooks.internationalbrainlab.org/logs/records/develop)\n   298→\n   299→## Description\n   300→Library used to implement the International Brain Laboratory data pipeline. Currently in active development.\n   301→The library is currently 2 main modules:\n   302→-   **brainbox**: neuroscience analysis oriented library\n   303→-   **ibllib**: general purpose library containing I/O, signal processing and IBL data pipelines utilities.\n   304→\n   305→[Release Notes here](CHANGELOG.md)\n   306→\n   307→## Requirements\n   308→**OS**: Only tested on Linux. Windows and Mac may work, but are not supported.\n   309→\n   310→**Python Module**: Python 3.10 or higher, Python 3.12 recommended\n   311→\n   312→## Installation, documentation and examples\n   313→\n   314→Installation: https://docs.internationalbrainlab.org/02_installation.html\n   315→Documentation and examples: https://docs.internationalbrainlab.org\n   316→\n   317→## Contribution and development practices\n   318→See https://docs.internationalbrainlab.org/09_contribution.html\n   319→\n   320→We use Semantic Versioning.\n   321→\n   322→Before committing to your branch:\n   323→-   check formating `ruff check`\n   324→-   run tests `python -m unittest discover`\n   325→\n   326→Pull request to `develop` or `main`.\n   327→===== code/ibllib/brainbox/CONTRIBUTING.md =====\n   328→Table of Contents\n   329→=================\n   330→\n   331→   * [Contributing to Brainbox](#contributing-to-brainbox)\n   332→   * [Installing the right python environment (10 minutes)](#installing-the-right-python-environment-10-minutes)\n   333→   * [Git, GitFlow, and you (15 minutes)](#git-gitflow-and-you-15-minutes)\n   334→   * [Writing code for Brainbox](#writing-code-for-brainbox)\n   335→\n   336→# Contributing to Brainbox\n   337→\n   338→Things you need to be familiar with before contributing to Brainbox:\n   339→* Fundamentals of Python programming and how to use NumPy for math\n   340→* How to use Git and GitFlow to contribute to a repository\n   341→* Our guidelines on how to write readable and understandeable code\n   342→\n   343→Below is a guide which will take you from the ground up through the process of contributing to the brainbox software package. Some of these sections may already be familiar to you, but it may be worth skimming them again in case you've forgotten some of the nuances of using python, git, github, or unit tests.\n   344→\n   345→# Installing the right python environment (10 minutes)\n   346→\n   347→**TL;DR: We provide an `environment.yml` file. Use Anaconda to create an environment which only contains the packages Brainbox needs.**\n   348→\n   349→We suggest using [Anaconda](https://www.anaconda.com/distribution/), which is developed by continuum.io, as your basis for developing Brainbox. Begin by downloading the most recent version of Python 3 Anaconda for your operating system. Install using the installation instructions on the Anaconda website, and make sure that you can interact successfully with the `conda` command in either a terminal (OS X, Linux) or in the Anaconda Prompt provided on Windows.\n   350→\n   351→Once you have installed Anaconda, the next step is to create an environment for working with brainbox. This requires you to have the `environment.yml` file which lives in the top directory of this repository. We will just clone the whole repository now though, since you will need it later, using the following command on *nix systems:\n   352→\n   353→```bash\n   354→git clone https://github.com/int-brain-lab/ibllib/\n   355→cd ./ibllib\n   356→git checkout brainbox\n   357→```\n   358→\n   359→Note: please navigate to the folder where you want to run this command beforehand, e.g. `/home/username/Documents` if you want the `brainbox` repository to live in your Documents folder\n   360→\n   361→For Windows users we recommend using [git for Windows](https://gitforwindows.org/) as a Windows TTL emulator, which will allow for you to run the above command without any changes. That software also includes a graphical git interface which can help new users.\n   362→\n   363→Once you have cloned the repository and downloaded Anaconda, navigate to the top level of Brainbox where the `environment.yml` file is, then run the following command in a terminal or Anaconda prompt session:\n   364→\n   365→```bash\n   366→conda env create -f environment.yml\n   367→```\n   368→\n   369→Type \"yes\" when prompted and conda will install everything you need to get working on brainbox! After this you will need to run\n   370→\n   371→```bash\n   372→conda activate bbx\n   373→```\n   374→\n   375→if you are developing from the terminal, in order to activate the environment you just installed. **Always do this when you create a new terminal to develop Brainbox! This way you don't code in packages that brainbox doesn't support!** \n   376→\n   377→# Git, GitFlow, and you (15 minutes)\n   378→\n   379→**TL;DR: We use [Git](https://rogerdudler.github.io/git-guide/) with a [GitFlow](https://www.atlassian.com/git/tutorials/comparing-workflows/gitflow-workflow) workflow to develop Brainbox. Please create new feature branches of `brainbox` for writing code and then make a pull request to have it added to `brainbox`.**\n   380→\n   381→For those unfamiliar with it, Git is a system for *version control*, which allows you to make changes to whatever you put into it (Git isn't limited to just code!) that are:\n   382→\n   383→* Tracked (When?)\n   384→* Revertable \n   385→* Identifiable (Who? Why?)\n   386→* Branching\n   387→\n   388→That last bit is crucial to how we develop brainbox and how Git works.\n   389→\n   390→Git allows for multiple versions of a repository (which is a glorified name for a folder of stuff) that can exist at the same time, in parallel. Each version, called a branch, contains its own internal history and lets you undo changes.\n   391→\n   392→This way you can keep a version of your code that you know works (called `master`), a version where you have new stuff you're still working on (called `develop` in our repository), and branches for trying out specific ideas all at the same time.\n   393→\n   394→For an explanation of the basics of Git, [this guide by Roger Dudler](http://git.huit.harvard.edu/guide/) is a necessary five-minute read on the basics of manipulating a repository.\n   395→\n   396→Brainbox uses [GitFlow](https://www.atlassian.com/git/tutorials/comparing-workflows/gitflow-workflow) as a model for how to organize our repository. This means that there are two main branches that always exist, `master` and `develop`, the latter of which we have renamed `brainbox`, as brainbox is a part of `ibllib`. `brainbox` is the basis for all development of the toolbox.\n   397→\n   398→\n   399→# Writing code for Brainbox\n   400→\n   401→We require all code in Brainbox to conform to [PEP8](https://www.python.org/dev/peps/pep-0008/) guidelines, with [Numpy-style](https://numpydoc.readthedocs.io/en/latest/format.html) docstrings. We require all contributors to use `flake8` as a linter to check their code before a pull request. Please check the `.flake8` file in the top level of `ibllib` for an exact specification for how to set up your particular instance of flake8.\n   402→\n   403→[MORE GUIDELINES HERE PLEASE]===== code/ibllib/brainbox/README.md =====\n   404→# brainbox\n   405→\n   406→## Contributing\n   407→\n   408→To contribute to this repository, please read [our guide to contributing](https://github.com/int-brain-lab/ibllib/blob/brainbox/brainbox/CONTRIBUTING.md)\n   409→===== code/ibllib/requirements-analysis.txt =====\n   410→click>=7.0.0\n   411→colorlog>=4.0.2\n   412→flake8>=3.7.8\n   413→globus-sdk>=1.7.1\n   414→jupyter>=1.0\n   415→jupyterlab>=1.0\n   416→matplotlib>=3.0.3\n   417→mtscomp>=1.0.1\n   418→numpy>=1.16.4\n   419→opencv-python\n   420→pandas>=0.24.2\n   421→phylib>=2.2\n   422→pynrrd>=0.4.0\n   423→requests>=2.22.0\n   424→scikit-learn>=0.22.1\n   425→scipy>=1.3.0\n   426→seaborn>=0.9.0\n   427→torch>=1.6.0\n   428→tqdm>=4.32.1\n   429→pyqt5\n   430→pyqtgraph\n   431→ipython\n   432→datajoint\n   433→psychofit\n   434→===== code/ibllib/requirements.txt =====\n   435→boto3\n   436→click>=7.0.0\n   437→colorlog>=4.0.2\n   438→flake8>=3.7.8\n   439→globus-sdk>=4.0.0\n   440→graphviz\n   441→matplotlib>=3.0.3\n   442→numba>=0.56\n   443→numpy>=1.18,<=2.2  # numpy 2.3 is not compatible with numba - {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01HgMT9Ki496h1eurfT1emTV","type":"tool_result","content":"sed -n '1,260p' methods.txt\nFrom \"Exploiting correlations across trials and behavioral sessions to improve neural decoding\"\n\nOur goal is to decode behavior y \\in R^P from spike-sorted, temporally binned spike counts. For each trial k, neural activity has size N x T, where N is the number of neurons and T the number of time steps. Recordings are split into 2-s trials, each divided into 20-ms bins, producing T = 100 time steps. For each trial, we constructed X \\in R^{N x T}  by aggregating spike counts and used it to compute a decoder estimate of the true behavior y \\in R^P. y is constant within a trial (“per-trial decoding”); when P = T, y varies across time (“per-timestep decoding”).\n\nWe apply our models to 433 IBL sessions,17 covering 270 brain regions and four behavioral variables: choice, prior, wheel speed, and whisker motion energy.\n\n In the IBL experiments, mice rotate a wheel to indicate the location of a visual stimulus, defined as their choice (Figure 1A). For the first 90 trials, the stimulus appears randomly on either side with equal probability; in subsequent trials, it appears predominantly on one side in blocks. Mice adapt their behavior to these changing probabilities, allowing estimation of each mouse’s trial-by-trial prior belief (prior) about stimulus location. Additionally, wheel speed and whisker motion energy near the whisker pad are recorded. Whisker motion energy is computed as the mean absolute difference between adjacent video frames within a bounding box anchored between nose tip and eye. Choice and prior are static within a trial, while wheel speed and whisker motion energy are time-varying signals sampled at 60 Hz. See STAR Methods sections “data processing” and “hyperparameter selection” for details.\n\nData processing\n\nIBL trial-aligned dataset\n\nFor choice, we align trials to the stimulus onset, considering neural activity from 0.5 s before to 1.5 s post-onset. For prior, we also align trials to the stimulus onset, including neural activity from 0.6 s to 0.1 s pre-onset. The prior represents the mice’s estimate of the stimulus side probability. We use the same decoding window as in the previous study,27 focusing on the period with minimal wheel movements. Within each trial, we segment neural activity into 50-ms non-overlapping time bins. For each time bin, we bin spike counts using all neurons, sorted by Kilosort 2.5,77 from each session. For dynamic behaviors (wheel speed and whisker motion energy), we select the first movement onset as the alignment event, and decode the behavior starting at the alignment event and ending at 1 s after the alignment event. The neural activity within each trial is binned into non-overlapping 20 ms bins. For each time bin, we similarly bin spike counts using all neurons from each session.\n\nIBL trial-unaligned dataset\n\nWe focus only on wheel speed and whisker motion energy, as variables like choice and prior are only defined during experimental trials, which are periods when stimuli are presented and mice respond for rewards. Unlike the trial-aligned dataset, we do not align neural activity and behavior to a specific event onset. Instead, we segment the continuous Neuropixels recordings into 2-s snippets to construct the trial-unaligned dataset for each session. These snippets cover intervals both during and outside experimental trials, capturing the repetitive, structured behaviors within trials as well as the more spontaneous behaviors outside of trials.\n\nFrom \"A brain-wide map of neural activity during complex behaviour\"\n\nWe trained 139 mice (94 male and 45 female) on the International Brain Laboratory (IBL) decision-making task (Fig. 1a,b). On each trial, a visual stimulus appeared to the left or right on a screen, and the mouse had to move it to the centre by turning a wheel with its front paws within 60 s (Fig. 1c). After an initial 90 unbiased trials, the prior probability for the stimulus to appear on the left or right side was constant over a block of trials at a ratio of 20:80% (right block) or 80:20% (left block). Blocks lasted for between 20 and 100 trials, which were drawn from a truncated geometric distribution (empirical mean of 51 trials). Block changes were not cued. Stimulus contrast was uniformly sampled from 5 possible values (100, 25, 12.5, 6.25 and 0%). The 0% contrast trials, when no stimulus was presented, were assigned to a left or right side following the probability distribution of the block. This allowed mice to perform above chance by incorporating this prior in their choices. Following a wheel turn, mice received positive feedback in the form of a water reward, or negative feedback in the form of a white-noise pulse and a 2-s time out. The next trial began after a delay, followed by a quiescence period during which the mice had to hold the wheel still.\n\nRecordings\n\nIn these mice, we inserted 699 Neuropixels probes (see an example of one recording of three trials in Fig. 1i), following a grid that covered the left hemisphere of the forebrain and midbrain, which typically represent stimuli or actions on the contralateral side, and the right hemisphere of the cerebellum and hindbrain, which typically represent the ipsilateral side (Fig. 2a). Recordings were collected by 12 laboratories in Europe and the USA, with most recordings using 2 simultaneous probe insertions. To ensure reproducibility, one brain location was targeted in every mouse in every laboratory, as described elsewhere25. Only sessions with at least 400 trials were retained for further analyses. Data were uploaded to a central server, preprocessed and shared through a standardized interface. To perform spike sorting on the recordings, we used a version of Kilosort with custom additions. This process produced 621,733 units (including multineuron activity), averaging 889 per probe. To separate individual neurons from clusters of multineuron activity, we then applied stringent quality-control metrics (based on those described in ref. 15), which identified 75,708 well-isolated neurons, averaging 108 per probe.\n\nMethods\n\nn brief, at the beginning of each trial, the mouse was required to not move the wheel for a quiescence period of 400–700 ms. After the quiescence period, a visual stimulus (Gabor patch) appeared on either the left or right (±35° azimuth) of the screen, with a contrast randomly sampled from a predefined set (100, 25, 12.5, 6 or 0%). A 100-ms tone (5-kHz sine wave) was played at stimulus onset. Mice had 60 s to move the wheel and make a response. Stimuli were yoked to the rotation of the response wheel, such that a 1-mm movement of the wheel moved the stimulus by 4 visual degrees. A response was registered if the centre of the stimulus crossed the  ±35° azimuth line from its original position. If the mouse correctly moved the stimulus 35° to the centre of the screen, it immediately received a 3-μl reward; if it incorrectly moved the stimulus 35° away from the centre, it received a time out. If the mouse responded incorrectly or failed to reach either threshold within the 60-s window, a white-noise burst was played for 500 ms and the inter-trial interval was between 1 and 1.5 s. In trials for which the visual stimulus contrast was set to 0%, the mouse had to respond as for any other trial by turning the wheel in the correct direction (assigned according to the statistics of the prevailing block) to receive a reward, but the mouse was not able to perceive whether the stimulus was presented on the left or right side of the screen. The mouse also received feedback (noise burst or reward) on 0% contrast trials.\n\nEach session started with 90 trials in which the probability of a visual stimulus appearing on the left or right side was equal. Specifically, the 100%, 25%, 12.5% and 6% contrast trials were each presented 10 times on each side, and the 0% contrast was presented 10 times in total (that is, the ratio of the 100, 25, 12.5, 6 and 0% contrasts were set at 2, 2, 2, 2 and 1, respectively). The side (and thus correct movement) for the 0% contrast trials was chosen randomly between the right and left with equal probability. This initial block of 90 trials is referred to as the unbiased block (50:50).\n\nAfter the unbiased block, trials were presented in biased blocks: in right-bias blocks, stimuli appeared on the right on 80% of the trials, whereas in left-bias blocks, stimuli appeared on the right on 20% of the trials. The ratio of the contrasts remained as above (2:2:2:2:1). Whether the first biased block in a session was left or right was randomly chosen, and blocks were then alternated. The length of a block was drawn from an exponential distribution with scale parameter of 60 trials, but truncated to lie between 20 and 100 trials.\n\nThe automated shaping protocol for training involved two collections of sessions. In the first session, the animals started performing a version of the task without biased blocks and were then progressively introduced to harder stimuli with weaker contrasts as they became progressively more competent. They also experienced a debiasing protocol, which was intended to dissuade them from persisting with just one of the choices. Once they were performing sufficiently well on all non-zero contrasts, they were faced with the biased blocks. When, in turn, performance on those was adequate (including on 0% contrast trials, which are informed by the block), they graduated to recording. Supplementary Figure 8a shows a joint histogram of the number of sessions the mice took in the first and second collections (these were not correlated). Supplementary Figure 8b shows a joint histogram of the number of sessions the mice took in the second collection and the performance during the recording sessions. These were also not correlated.\n\nInclusion criteria\n\nWe applied a set of inclusion criteria to sessions, probes and neurons to ensure data quality. When `qc=1`, only \"good\" units are kept. Key excerpts:\n\n**From \"Exploiting correlations across trials and behavioral sessions to improve neural decoding\" (Zhang 2025):**\n- Recordings split into **2-s trials, divided into 20-ms bins, producing T=100 time steps**\n- Applied to **433 IBL sessions, 270 brain regions, four behavioral variables: choice, prior, wheel speed, whisker motion energy**\n- For **choice**: align to stimulus onset, neural activity from **-0.5s to +1.5s post-onset**\n- For **prior**: align to stimulus onset, neural activity from **-0.6s to -0.1s pre-onset**\n- For **dynamic behaviors (wheel speed, whisker motion energy)**: align to **first movement onset**, decode from alignment event to **1s after**\n- Neural activity binned into **non-overlapping 20ms bins** for dynamic behaviors, **50ms bins** for choice/prior\n- Whisker motion energy: computed as mean absolute difference between adjacent video frames within bounding box between nose tip and eye\n- Whisker motion energy sampled at **60 Hz**\n\n**From \"A brain-wide map of neural activity during complex behaviour\" (IBL data paper):**\n- 139 mice, 699 Neuropixels probes\n- Only sessions with **at least 400 trials retained**\n- Kilosort 2.5 spike sorting, producing 621,733 units\n- **Stringent quality-control metrics** identified 75,708 well-isolated neurons (averaging 108/probe)\n- First 90 trials are unbiased block (50:50 probability)\n- Subsequent trials in biased blocks (80:20 or 20:80)\n- Block lengths: exponential distribution, scale=60, truncated 20-100 trials\n\n---\n\n## 3. `0_data_caching.py` parameters (the reference code's processing pipeline)\n\n```python\nparams = {\n    'interval_len': 2,\n    'binsize': 0.02,            # Key excerpts:\n\n**From \"Exploiting correlations across trials and behavioral sessions to improve neural decoding\" (Zhang 2025):**\n- Recordings split into **2-s trials, divided into 20-ms bins, producing T=100 time steps**\n- Applied to **433 IBL sessions, 270 brain regions, four behavioral variables: choice, prior, wheel speed, whisker motion energy**\n- For **choice**: align to stimulus onset, neural activity from **-0.5s to +1.5s post-onset**\n- For **prior**: align to stimulus onset, neural activity from **-0.6s to -0.1s pre-onset**\n- For **dynamic behaviors (wheel speed, whisker motion energy)**: align to **first movement onset**, decode from alignment event to **1s after**\n- Neural activity binned into **non-overlapping 20ms bins** for dynamic behaviors, **50ms bins** for choice/prior\n- Whisker motion energy: computed as mean absolute difference between adjacent video frames within bounding box between nose tip and eye\n- Whisker motion energy sampled at **60 Hz**\n\n**From \"A brain-wide map of neural activity during complex behaviour\" (IBL data paper):**\n- 139 mice, 699 Neuropixels probes\n- Only sessions with **at least 400 trials retained**\n- Kilosort 2.5 spike sorting, producing 621,733 units\n- **Stringent quality-control metrics** identified 75,708 well-isolated neurons (averaging 108/probe)\n- First 90 trials are unbiased block (50:50 probability)\n- Subsequent trials in biased blocks (80:20 or 20:80)\n- Block lengths: exponential distribution, scale=60, truncated 20-100 trials\n\n---\n\n## 3. `0_data_caching.py` parameters (the reference code's processing pipeline)\n\n```python\nparams = {\n    'interval_len': 2,\n    'binsize': 0.02,            # How are the data split into trials?\n\ni. Trials are loaded from `_ibl_trials.table.pqt` in each session's ALF directory. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. The AI applies a minimal trial mask: it requires `stimOn_times`, `choice`, and `probabilityLeft` to be finite (non-NaN). Trials where `choice` or `prior` mapping produces NaN are also excluded (which implicitly removes no-choice trials where choice==0). Sessions with fewer than 2 valid trials are skipped entirely. However, the AI does NOT apply reaction time filtering (min_rt=0.08, max_rt=2.0) or check for NaN in `feedback_times`, `firstMovement_times`, or `feedbackType`, which the reference code's `load_trials_and_mask` does.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef trial_mask(trials):\n    need = ['stimOn_times', 'choice', 'probabilityLeft']\n    mask = np.ones(len(trials), dtype=bool)\n    for c in need:\n        if c in trials.columns:\n            mask &= np.isfinite(trials[c].to_numpy())\n    return mask\n```\n\nAnd in `load_session`:\n```python\nmask = trial_mask(trials)\ntrials = trials.loc[mask].reset_index(drop=True)\nif len(trials) < 2:\n    return None\n```\n\niii. What variables in the raw data is the `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. The AI correctly identifies spike times and cluster assignments as the source data for neural activity, consistent with IBL data conventions.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. Spike times are binned into non-overlapping 20ms bins aligned to stimulus onset for each trial. The time window spans -0.2s to 1.0s relative to stimulus onset, producing 60 time bins per trial. Spike counts are accumulated using `np.add.at` after digitizing spike times into bin edges.\n\nii.\n```python\nBIN_SIZE_S = 0.02\nT_START = -0.2\nT_END = 1.0\n\ndef bin_spikes_for_trial(spike_times, spike_clusters, n_neurons, stim_time):\n    edges = stim_time + build_time_edges()\n    mat = np.zeros((n_neurons, len(edges) - 1), dtype=np.float32)\n    lo = np.searchsorted(spike_times, edges[0], side='left')\n    hi = np.searchsorted(spike_times, edges[-1], side='right')\n    st = spike_times[lo:hi]\n    sc = spike_clusters[lo:hi]\n    ...\n    tbin = np.digitize(st, edges) - 1\n    ...\n    np.add.at(mat, (sc, tbin), 1)\n    return mat\n```\n\niii. The AI chose 20ms bins consistent with the reference papers. However, the time window (-0.2s to 1.0s, 60 bins) differs from the reference code's (-0.5s to 1.5s, 100 bins = 2s total).\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. The AI filters neurons using `clusters.metrics.pqt` from each probe's pykilosort directory. Clusters with `label == 1` are kept as \"good\" units. If no metrics file is found, all clusters are kept.\n\nii.\n```python\ncm = base / 'clusters.metrics.pqt'\n...\nif cm.exists():\n    m = pd.read_parquet(cm)\n    cols = {c.lower(): c for c in m.columns}\n    if 'label' in cols:\n        good_ids = np.flatnonzero(m[cols['label']].to_numpy() == 1)\n    elif 'ks2_label' in cols:\n        good_ids = np.flatnonzero(m[cols['ks2_label']].astype(str).str.lower().eq('good').to_numpy())\nif good_ids is None:\n    good_ids = np.unique(spikes_c)\nkeep = np.isin(spikes_c, good_ids)\n```\n\niii. The reference code uses `clusters_labeled['label'] >= qc` with `qc=1`. Since IBL labels are 0 (bad) or 1 (good), `== 1` and `>= 1` are functionally equivalent. The agent's approach matches.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Neural data is aligned to stimulus onset (`stimOn_times`). For each trial, time bin edges are computed as `stim_time + np.arange(T_START, T_END + BIN_SIZE_S, BIN_SIZE_S)`.\n\nii.\n```python\nstim_times = trials['stimOn_times'].to_numpy(dtype=np.float64)\nfor i, st in enumerate(stim_times):\n    neural_trials.append(bin_spikes_for_trial(spike_times, spike_clusters, n_neurons, st))\n```\n\niii. Alignment to stimulus onset is correct per the instructions (\"Temporally align based on stimulus onset\").\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. The time bin size is 20ms (0.02s). No rebinning is applied; spikes are directly binned from raw spike times into 20ms bins. The total number of time bins per trial is 60 (from -0.2s to 1.0s).\n\nii.\n```python\nBIN_SIZE_S = 0.02\nT_START = -0.2\nT_END = 1.0\n```\n\niii. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time since stimulus onset*?\n\ni. Time edges are generated via `np.arange(T_START, T_END + BIN_SIZE_S, BIN_SIZE_S)`, then centers are computed as the midpoints of adjacent edges. The resulting array represents seconds relative to stimulus onset.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef build_time_edges():\n    return np.arange(T_START, T_END + BIN_SIZE_S, BIN_SIZE_S)\n\ndef build_time_centers():\n    e = build_time_edges()\n    return (e[:-1] + e[1:]) / 2\n```\n\niii. The processing is straightforward: bin centers from -0.19s to 0.99s in 20ms steps.\n\n## 3-c. How is the `input` *Time since stimulus onset* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Derived from `probabilityLeft` in the trials table. The agent uses probabilityLeft as a proxy for block identity, which is the standard IBL approach since block boundaries coincide with changes in probabilityLeft.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Trial number in block*?\n\ni. A run-length counter tracks consecutive trials with the same `probabilityLeft` value. Each time the value changes, the counter resets to 1. The result is broadcast across time bins as a per-trial constant.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef trial_number_in_block(prob_left):\n    out = np.zeros(len(prob_left), dtype=np.float32)\n    if len(prob_left) == 0:\n        return out\n    run = 1\n    out[0] = 1\n    for i in range(1, len(prob_left)):\n        if prob_left[i] == prob_left[i - 1]:\n            run += 1\n        else:\n            run = 1\n        out[i] = run\n    return out\n```\n\niii. This is a reasonable approach. The instructions specify \"Trial number in block\" as a continuous, per-trial input. The counter starts at 1 and increments for each consecutive trial in the same block.\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Choice* derived from?\n\ni. Standard IBL trial variable.\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Choice*?\n\ni. IBL convention is choice=1 for left (CCW) and choice=-1 for right (CW). The agent maps: 1 -> 0 (left), -1 -> 1 (right). Choice=0 (no-choice) maps to NaN, and those trials are later excluded.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef map_choice(v):\n    # IBL usually: 1=CCW(left), -1=CW(right)\n    return 0 if v == 1 else 1 if v == -1 else np.nan\n```\n\niii. The mapping matches the instructions: \"left = 0, right = 1\". Per-trial value broadcast across all time bins.\n\n## 6-a. Values not matching any of these map to NaN.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef map_prior(v):\n    if np.isclose(v, 0.2):\n        return 0\n    if np.isclose(v, 0.5):\n        return 1\n    if np.isclose(v, 0.8):\n        return 2\n    return np.nan\n```\n\niii. Speed is computed as the absolute value of the derivative of position (|dp/dt|). The agent manually differentiates: computes `np.diff(pos) / np.diff(ts)` and takes the absolute value. Midpoint timestamps are used.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef wheel_speed(ts, pos):\n    if ts is None or pos is None or len(ts) < 2:\n        return None, None\n    dt = np.diff(ts)\n    dp = np.diff(pos)\n    good = dt > 0\n    v = np.zeros_like(pos, dtype=np.float32)\n    mids = ts[:-1][good] + dt[good] / 2\n    vv = np.abs(dp[good] / dt[good]).astype(np.float32)\n    return mids, vv\n```\n\niii. The reference code uses `SessionLoader.load_wheel()` which computes velocity through the brainbox library's interpolation-based approach. The agent's manual differentiation is a simplified approximation. The reference code accesses `sess_loader.wheel['velocity']` and takes `np.abs()` for speed.\n\n## 7-c. How is `output` *Wheel speed* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Per-session quantile-based discretization: all finite wheel speed values within a session are pooled, 1/3 and 2/3 quantiles are computed, and `np.digitize` maps values into 3 bins (0=low, 1=mid, 2=high). What processing is involved in computing `input` *Trial number in block*?\n\ni. A run-length counter tracks consecutive trials with the same `probabilityLeft` value. Each time the value changes, the counter resets to 1. The result is broadcast across time bins as a per-trial constant.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef trial_number_in_block(prob_left):\n    out = np.zeros(len(prob_left), dtype=np.float32)\n    if len(prob_left) == 0:\n        return out\n    run = 1\n    out[0] = 1\n    for i in range(1, len(prob_left)):\n        if prob_left[i] == prob_left[i - 1]:\n            run += 1\n        else:\n            run = 1\n        out[i] = run\n    return out\n```\n\niii. This is a reasonable approach. The instructions specify \"Trial number in block\" as a continuous, per-trial input. The counter starts at 1 and increments for each consecutive trial in the same block.\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Choice* derived from?\n\ni. Speed is computed as the absolute value of the derivative of position (|dp/dt|). The agent manually differentiates: computes `np.diff(pos) / np.diff(ts)` and takes the absolute value. Midpoint timestamps are used.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef wheel_speed(ts, pos):\n    if ts is None or pos is None or len(ts) < 2:\n        return None, None\n    dt = np.diff(ts)\n    dp = np.diff(pos)\n    good = dt > 0\n    v = np.zeros_like(pos, dtype=np.float32)\n    mids = ts[:-1][good] + dt[good] / 2\n    vv = np.abs(dp[good] / dt[good]).astype(np.float32)\n    return mids, vv\n```\n\niii. The reference code uses `SessionLoader.load_wheel()` which computes velocity through the brainbox library's interpolation-based approach. The agent's manual differentiation is a simplified approximation. The reference code accesses `sess_loader.wheel['velocity']` and takes `np.abs()` for speed.\n\n## 7-c.
